Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 8

Affordability Agenda: Healthcare, Housing, and 
Education Loans  
The affordability agenda has become a centerpiece of many Democratic as well 
as Republican campaign platforms.  This platform is impacting investor 
debates across healthcare, housing, and student loans. In healthcare,  the push 
among some Democrats for expanded health coverage under the Medicare for All 
banner is placing pressure on managed care stocks, but meanwhile, companies 
with exposure to Medicaid and  the Affordable Care Act (ACA) could see a 
supportive political backdrop. On housing, investor debates on the impacts of the 
affordability agenda have continued since passage of legislation in July 2026 that 
caps the number of single -family homes for -profit institutional investors can 
purchase. Affordability of higher education is likewise part of the conversatio n, 
leading investors to debate which levers Congress could pull that would affect 
student loan companies.  
Healthcare 
In healthcare, w e see the upcoming midterm elections as a sentiment event for 
managed care stocks , not a policy event.  With the presidential veto in place 
through 2028, we do not expect the government to enact direct healthcare 
legislation during the next Congress. Forecasts place the probability of a change 
in House control at roughly 79%, with both chambers changing in about 44% of 
simulations. Although investor skittishness around Medicare for All (M4A) rhetoric 
is high, we see the likelihood of M4A replacing private coverage as a near -zero 
probability in this timeframe.  
M4A has no realistic legislative path before 2029, but the rhetoric weighs on 
managed care organization (MCO) stock multiples by raising perceived long-term 
risk rather than affecting near -term earnings. Polling from  Kaiser Family 
Foundation, a health policy research organization, shows support for M4A at a 
decade-plus high, and the 2018 –2019 cycle suggests that increased campaign 
attention can pressure MCO multiples even without a direct policy impact. We 
expect similar sentiment -driven pressure through year -end and potentially into 
the 2028 primary cycle but would not view it as a change i n near -term 
fundamentals.  
For Medicare Advantage, we see a h eadline risk over earnings reality. The near-
term earnings impact for affected companies should be limited, because the US 
Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) has finalized a 2.48% Medicare 
Advantage (MA) rate increase for 2027, versus 0.09% in the advance notice. A 
change in House control would instead increase focus on 2028 payment policy, 
risk adj ustment, and prior authorization. Coding reform already has bipartisan 
support: sponsors estimate that the Cassidy -Merkley No UPCODE Act could 
generate up to $275  billion of savings over ten years, while MedPAC estimates 
coding intensity will add roughly $40  billion to 2025 Medicare Advantage  costs 
relative to traditional Medicare. In our view, the more immediate consideration is 
potential multiple pressure rather than a measurable change to company 
earnings.  
However, the next Congress could b enefit Medicaid funding and ACA policy. 
Medicaid and the ACA appear relatively better positioned, although any benefit

### Page 9

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 9

would likely be reflected first in sentiment and lower perceived policy risk rather 
than near -term estimate revisions. Medicaid community engagement 
requirements take effect in most states by January 1, 2027. Although repeal 
remains unlikely given the presidential veto, a change in House control could shift 
the year -end funding debate toward  restoring or  preserving federal Medicaid 
support, which could ease state budget pressure and support rate -setting. The 
ACA policy backdrop could also become more supportive, although we see 
limited scope for enhanced premium subsidies to return before 2029 after the 
three-year extension passed by the House stalled.  
Housing 
Turning to the housing sector, the likely midterm outcome of a split government 
would result in more gridlock. Historically, political gridlock in terms of passing 
new regulation has been a tailwind for the real estate sector, reducing regulatory 
risk at the federal level. In the scenario of a split Congress, in which the Democrats 
have a majority in the House but not the Senate, bills with bipartisan support have 
a greater likelihood to become law. Two key areas in which this bipartisan support 
exists are housing affordability and consumer protection. 
Among real estate investment trusts ( REITs), we see Residential as among the 
most impacted subsectors, particularly as it relates to the affordability debate. 
Regardless of the election outcome, housing affordability has become front and 
center on the political agenda  across both parties. F ederal legislation  is more 
likely to target supply -side solutions, as legislation restricting rent increases is 
typically handled at the local and state level. We think Multifamily would be the 
most likely subsector impacted, as a push to expand rental housing supply could 
lead to eased zoning restrictions, particularly in supply -constrained coastal 
markets that have historically benefited from higher barriers to entry. That said, 
this would likely take years to materialize. With a Democratic majority , Congress 
could voice opposition to the influence of large institutional landlords, but we see 
this strictly as a sentiment risk to stocks.  
Single Family For Rent (SFR) is less exposed to near -term legislative shocks, as 
the sector was a focus of the 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act passed in July 
2026 over the summer. The  measure impacts institutional owners’ ability to buy 
homes off the MLS but contains specific carve-out provisions that will allow them 
to keep developing build -to-rent (BTR) product and buy excess inventory from 
homebuilders, the sector's preferred acquis ition channel in recent years. The 
ROAD to Housing Act also contained provisions that could theoretically ease the 
production of manufactured homes, the main business segment for 
Manufactured Housing (MH) REITs. The first-order impact is likely more flexibility 
in home designs that could lead to further densification of MH communities, 
although we note emerging investor debate about the long -term impacts of 
increased MH production on competitive supply for a property type t hat has 
historically been supply constrained, mainly due to NIMBY-ism.  
Parallel to the conversation on affordability, it is clear that, regardless of which 
political party may be in power following the midterms, a major focus remains 
addressing the US fiscal deficit and its continued growth. We continue to pay 
attention to potential budget cuts or sequestration to slow government spending 
and reduce the deficit, which could have an impact on real estate.  Key areas to 
watch include cuts to healthcare spending (which would be a negative for skilled

### Page 10

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 10

nursing focused REITs, given Medicare is a major revenue source for their tenant 
base) and cuts that would impact Office REITs with large government exposure.  
We also worry that an elevated federal deficit and the elevated Treasury 
borrowing needs today create a risk that interest rates will remain  higher for 
longer. This puts pressure on real estate valuations and cost of financing for REITs 
to drive external growth via acquisitions and development. A split Congress is 
likely to result in more difficult budget negotiations and increased oversight, but 
it remains unclear w hat the ultimate outcome would be to address the budget 
deficit issue. 
With real estate being more dependent on local market dynamics, we believe the 
more likely regulatory impact would come from local regulation. This could show 
up on the ballots during the midterms , with increased possibility of rent control 
legislation targeted at residential real estate as well as self -storage, as cities and 
municipalities focus on affordability issues. There is also growing legislative 
pushback at the local level against development of real assets such as data 
centers given the large power requirements, as discussed in detail elsewhere in 
this report.  
Education Lending 
Midterm campaign platforms on education affordability  have a bearing on the 
consumer and specialty finance sector. Much of the policy exposure in this sector 
is through the executive branch bodies, not Congress, however. This includes, for 
example, regulatory authorities  such as the FDIC (Federal Deposit Insurance 
Corporation), the CFPB (Consumer Financial Protection Bureau ), or the FHFA 
(Federal Housing Finance Agency, which regulates Fannie Mae and Freddie 
Mac). That said, historical data shows that student loan company stocks can still 
directionally align with midterm party outcome and expectations  in Congress, 
although the sample size is small.

### Page 11

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 11

Mixed Impacts of Economic and Fiscal Policy  
Democratic and Republican candidates have aimed to distinguish themselves 
from their opponents along a range of economic and fiscal policy matters, 
including tariffs, tax, labor, and the budget. In reality, the outcome of the 
midterms, regardless of which party prevails, is likely to present a mixture of relief 
and restrictions affecting businesses. In some cases, investor fears over sweeping 
economic policy changes are overblown for topics over which Congress 
realistically has limited influence, presenting more headline  risk than a risk to 
fundamentals. In considering these, we look to industrials that are particularly 
sensitive to key investor debates , spanning multi-industry/electrical equipment, 
clean tech and alternative energy, transport, and defense.  
For the multi-industry and electrical equipment (MI/EE) sector, historical midterm 
election cycles suggest a generally constructive backdrop heading into election 
day, with positive returns in six of the past seven elections. On average, the MI/EE 
group has gained ~5% during the three months preceding historical midterm 
elections and ~8% during the final month prior. Relative outperformance v ersus 
the S&P 500 has been less consistent; we observed outperformance in only four 
of seven cycles, though the group still generated average excess returns of 
~250bps over the preceding three months and ~270bps over the preceding 
month.  
Overall, the data supports a favorable absolute return backdrop ahead of 
midterms, but significant stock -level dispersion suggests company -specific 
fundamentals and factor leadership have historically mattered more than the 
election cycle alone. We mapped out election results scenarios across key policy 
pillars for our coverage (tax policy and capital investment; trade, tariffs, and 
supply chains; energy, grid, and power infrastructure; AI and data center 
regulation; and the regulatory environment). We focus on two key scenarios: one 
in which Republicans maintain control of both chambers, and a divided 
government that sees the Democrats controlling one or both chambers of the 
legislative branch.  
Under a divided government, we expect stock -level dispersion would likely 
narrow, with globally exposed companies receiving some trade policy relief and 
HVAC/automation companies benefiting from a potentially more supportive 
regulatory backdrop. However, should Republicans maintain control of Congress, 
our scenario analysis suggests this would be favorable for electrical equipment 
and power infrastructure manufacturers, due to a combination of pro-investment 
tax policy, faster infrastructure deployment, and a lighter AI regulatory 
framework. That said, globally exposed companies could face incremental tariff 
and retaliation risks.  
Looking to the clean tech and alternative energy sector, the midterm elections 
could represent a positive tailwind . T he next Congress might be positioned to 
pursue targeted extensions or modifications to certain clean -energy tax credits. 
The introduction of H.R. 8477, which proposes to restore several clean energy tax 
credits rolled back in late 2025, seems to signal tha t some, albeit a limited 
number, of Republicans could be supportive of this initiative. If the Democrats 
gain a majority in the House but not the Senate in the midterms, it is plausible that 
Democrats could find sufficient  Republican support to advance

### Page 12

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 12

legislation extending clean -energy credits, though they would still face 
significant legislative hurdles. A midterm outcome in which Republicans retain 
control of both chambers is likely to maintain the status quo  for the remaining 
existing tax credits, especially for those tied to domestic manufacturing. While 
those could potentially see further extension, we think this is a limited outcome.    
While we see a low probability of a major change in, or support for, the residential 
tax credits that expired at the end of 2025 (25D), we envision a scenario in which 
indirect residential tax credits (48E) could be extended, which would help boost 
part of the demand. Softness  in demand is expected to remain going into 2027, 
still impacted by the phaseout of the 25D tax credits, the eventual roll-off of safe-
harbor benefits, and a more constrained financing environment.  
From a manufacturing tax credit standpoint,  there could be additional tailwinds  
if we were to see any manufacturing tax credit extension or positive modification 
(although lower probability, in our view) . This would also likely improve utility -
scale project economics if broader project -related credits are preserved, though 
upcoming elections could cause a slowdown in new booking activity until 
legislative uncertainty clears.  
For Transports,  the midterms would be most relevant for the trucking supply 
thesis. Truckload stocks could have a modest negative reaction to a Democratic 
majority if investors expect some rollback of the Trump administration’s 
constraints on drivers , which has tightened supply . We would separate 
perception from fundamentals, however. The election of a Democratic Congress 
would not automatically by itself change leadership at the Department of 
Transportation (DOT) and the Federal Motor Carrier Safety Administration 
(FMCSA), the executive branch entities driving much of the current push  for 
restrictions on drivers.  
One possible impact of  the midterms for  Transports is if  a change in 
Congressional control  contributed to a faster resolution of the war in Iran . Any 
resulting lower energy prices would be a positive for Transports . We view this as 
a lower -probability outcome,  however, given the constraints on a divided 
government.  
With clashes over the budget likely looming, aerospace and def ense could get 
caught in the crosshairs. W hile defense spending has historically enjoyed 
bipartisan support, it has grown politicized following the war in Iran, making it 
more difficult for a split Congress to pass such a budget. The defense budget has 
experienced significant growth over the last few years, rising from $900 billion in 
FY2024 to $1 trillion in FY2026. The Trump administration would like to see this 
trend accelerate: in April 2026, the president issued a budget request that called 
for a $1.5  trillion budget for the Department of Defense in FY 2027. With a 
continuing resolution likely into year-end, the outcome of the midterm elections 
will determine whether the next Congress can pass such a budget. If Republicans 
hold the House and Senate, we think there  is a reasonable prospect of a budget 
of $1.2 trillion or more  passing into law . Defense investors would see that as a 
substantial tailwind for beaten-up defense stocks. On the other hand, this would 
be unlikely in a split Congress, in our view.  Given all this, we see the outcome of 
the upcoming midterm elections as key to determining how US defense stocks 
will perform into year end and beyond.

### Page 13

23 October 2026 
The Theme Screen

Deutsche Bank Securities Inc. Page 13

Taking Stock of the Known Unknowns 
Although Republicans could maintain control of both chambers,  consensus is 
converging around polls pointing to a Democratic majority in the House  and 
possibly the Senate. The perceived certainty has allowed the investor debate 
related to midterms to focus not only on who will win but how they will govern. In 
some cases, headlines are misrepresenting what a Congress constrained by the 
executive will be able to accomplish . In other areas, legislation that is expected 
to be passed will need to be taken together with offsetting factors. Whatever the 
outcome, the political backdrop will continue to influence the market and 
investor debates in the year ahead.

Taiwan Earnings Call Weekly Recap - Is Earnings
Still Enough to Drive Stock Price? | GSTW
30 October 2026 | 4:17 PM Taiwan Standard Time
Robert Wang
Goldman Sachs (Asia) L.L.C. Taipei Branch
FICC & Equities
Short week but busy for Taiwan in terms of earnings. Index is only down 1.8% WTD,
though multiple large caps saw over 10% drawdown in this week alone. The elevated
level in Taiwan vs other regional markets still attracted much short interest without

much buying interest except from retail. Overall sentiment is not bearish, though
investors have turned much more defensive and hiding in sectors such as Financials,
Telecom, and TSMC.
For earnings, winners' momentum was short lived; instead of chasing into the beat,
many chose to further profit take on the strength (Unimicron, EMC/TUC, Landmark
on open right after their earnings). Companies that missed the results were definitely
punished (i.e. Chroma, Delta, UMC). Echoing back to my previous note, range bound

trade seems to be the safest way to stay afloat.
See below for my single stock takeaway:
Mediatek (2454 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$168.06bn (+10% QoQ and +18% YoY) with EPS at
NT$17.5 (+15% QoQ and +11% YoY) vs cons. NT$15.95. GM came in at 46.3%
(+11ppt QoQ and 18ppt YoY) vs cons. 46.2%.
3Q26 outperformance was mainly from stronger-than-expected customer
demand and improved supply chain yield. First ASIC already started shipping since

Sep'26.
Operational Outlook:
Revenue outlook for 4Q26 is NT$210 - NT$222bn, translating to a 29% sequential
growth. 2027 revenue growth will likely grow by high single digits yoy. GM will be
around 45% with +/-1.5%, and OPM is expected to be at 26% +/-2%.
ASIC chip sales for 2026 will be around US$2.5bn, higher than previous guidance,
and it will reach US$16bn in 2027, around 20% of ASIC TAM and at the higher end
of the company guidance. Next gen chip's tape out is on track and targeted to

enter mass production in 1Q28.
Delta Electronics (2308 TT)

Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$197.1bn (+8% QoQ / +31% YoY), while EPS registered
at NT$9.05. GM declined to 34.1% (down 1.5ppt QoQ), impacted by a NT$3.9bn
inventory write-down (~1.7% of revenue) mostly in Power Electronics.
Operating profit rose +7% QoQ (+31% YoY) backed by operating leverage, keeping
OPM resilient at 16.6% flat QoQ. Net profit was NT$23.5bn (-7% QoQ / +26% YoY)
due to lower non-operating investment gains.
Operational Outlook:

Management expects 4Q26 revenue to grow sequentially with gross margins
unlikely to deteriorate, aided by inventory normalization and potential price
adjustments to offset ~2% YTD material cost inflation.
AI-related exposure approaches 50% of total revenue (liquid cooling at ~13%), and
management remains confident in AI infrastructure demand through 2030 despite
near-term bottlenecks.
Key Q&A:
Inventory Write-down & Normalization: The NT$3.9bn write-down stemmed from

component mismatches and slower customer ramp-ups of newer platforms (e.g.,
18.5kW vs. 5.5kW platforms); management expects affected inventory to be
progressively consumed by 1Q27.
HVDC Adoption & Vicor Litigation: While 400V products saw partial shipments in
3Q26, 800V adoption is slower and primarily NVIDIA-driven. On the Vicor VPD
patent dispute, management expects no near-term impact on customer
relationships or shipments, with an initial ITC determination unlikely until 2027.

ASE Technology Holding (3711 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 consolidated revenue reached NT$244.16bn, with a GM of 22.1% and EPS of
NT$5.84, all outperforming market expectations.
ATM (Advanced Semiconductor Engineering, Test and Materials) GM hit 30.6%,
breaking past previous structural highs; the company announced 30% as its
baseline gross margin for the future.
Operational Outlook:
4Q26 consolidated revenue is projected to grow 6%–8% QoQ, with GM between

23.0%–24.0% and OPM between 14.5%–15.5%.
CPO is set to enter mass production in 4Q26, with related revenue expected to
contribute over US$500mn in 2027; the full-year LEAP revenue target has been
revised upward to over US$4bn.
Key Q&A:
Capex Upward Revision: Due to strong demand for LEAP and mainstream
advanced packaging, full-year 2026 capex was raised from US$10.5bn to
US$11.5bn (60% allocated to LEAP, 40% to mainstream packaging), with 2027
capex expected to be even higher.

Capacity Bottlenecks & Expansion: Factory floor space is currently the primary
constraint on near-term growth. The company is actively searching for and
acquiring existing facilities to shorten setup timelines, prioritizing resource
allocation toward packaging and wafer sort.

Financial Highlights:
Driven by strong demand, actual 3Q26 revenue grew 29% QoQ, comfortably
beating prior conservative expectations.
GM outperformed expectations, hitting 37.7% for the quarter.
Operational Outlook:
4Q26 revenue growth is conservatively estimated to be at least 15%–20% QoQ,
with gross margins expanding further to 38.0%–39.5%.
Proactive capacity expansion plans (targeting 15.25 million sheets/month by end-
2030 vs. 5.85 million sheets/month currently) are still insufficient to support all

signed Long-Term Agreements (LTAs) for the next 4 years, pointing to a severe
supply-demand gap and zero oversupply risk.
Key Q&A:
CCL Pricing & Substrate Expansion: EMC is executing its second round of high-end
CCL (M7+) price hikes this year (5%–20% increases covering M7/M8 and M8+/M9
grades). Furthermore, it is rapidly expanding high-end ABF substrate core-layer
CCL capacity from 300k sheets/month to at least 1.2 million sheets/month by end-
2027, targeting commercial shipments starting in 4Q27.

Competition & Market Share: While AI customers are qualifying second or third
sources to secure supply chains, EMC maintains its long-term target of holding a
50% AI CCL market share, supported by best-in-class product quality and deep
CSP/PCB collaboration.
Yageo Corporation (2327 TT)
Financial Highlights:
3Q26 revenue reached NT$49.53bn (+11.4% QoQ, +49.7% YoY), with a GM of 41%
and EPS of NT$5.83.
Free cash flow surged to NT$9.236 billion, while the net financial debt-to-EBITDA

ratio dropped from 59% in 4Q25 to 24% in 3Q26, reflecting a continuously
improving financial structure and robust cash generation.
Operational Outlook:
4Q26 revenue, GM, and OPM are all expected to edge up sequentially, defying
typical seasonal flat-to-down trends for the fourth quarter.
AI demand remains solid, with its contribution to total revenue expanding from
15% in 1Q26 and 16% in 2Q26 to approximately 20% in 3Q26.
Key Q&A:
Capacity & Expansion Plans: Capacity utilization rates stood at 95% in 3Q26 and

are expected to exceed 95% in 4Q26, surpassing the healthy range of 85%–90%.
Total capacity for tantalum capacitors and MLCCs is planned to expand by 10%–
15% in 2027, with portions coming online starting in the second quarter.
Order Momentum & B/B Ratio: The overall book-to-bill (B/B) ratio in 3Q26
remained close to 2.0, with AI-related tantalum capacitors and MLCCs seeing
particularly robust orders, while LTAs with core customers continue to underpin

future shipments.

realize each day a loss or a gain which is a multiple (for leveraged ETFs and ETNs) or the inverse (for inverse ETFs and ETNs) of the

performance on that day of their underlying index or benchmark. For this reason, if they are held for a period longer than one day, their
performance over such longer periods of time can differ significantly from the stated multiple of the performance (or the inverse of the
performance) of their underlying index or benchmark during the same period of time. This effect can be magnified in volatile markets.
Prior to entering into a transaction in leveraged or inverse ETFs, you should be aware of the general risks associated with such

transactions. You should not enter into leveraged or inverse ETFs transactions unless you understand the nature and extent of your risk
exposure. You should also be satisfied that the leveraged or inverse ETFs transaction is appropriate for you in light of your circumstances
and financial condition. ETFs and ETNs that are linked to commodity futures do not offer direct exposure to the commodity's spot price,
and may perform differently than the spot price for the commodity itself. Performance differential can be magnified if a specific

condition persists in the market for a commodity that creates a disparity between near-term future prices and long-term future prices
and may lead to unexpected performance results. Other factors, such as roll yield, transaction costs, management fees, and taxes may
cause deviation in performance between the spot price of a commodity and commodity futures. You should not assume that an ETF or
ETN that is linked to commodity futures will provide an effective hedge because of a negative correlation with equities or other asset

swaps, and other derivatives as well as non-investment-grade securities - give rise to substantial risk and are not available to nor suitable
for all investors. If you have any questions about whether you are eligible to enter into these transactions with Goldman Sachs, please

• The global landscape remains complex, shaped by AI, 
energy prices and tariffs. 
• Taiwan’s AI cycle remains strong, with robust AI 
exports and investment and an intact semiconductor 
competitive moat. 
• AI is generating broader domestic spillovers, with 
wealth effects and wage growth supporting 
consumption. 
• Taiwan is cushioning the energy shock by diversifying 
energy imports and absorbing much of the increase in 
upstream costs. 
• AI-driven growth is likely to moderate from 4Q26 into 
2027, as hyperscaler  cash-flow pressures rise and 
higher global rates lift investment costs. 
• Energy remains a n inflation risk, with domestic 
electricity prices facing upward pressure after the 
November local elections. 
• GDP growth is expected to moderate to high -single 
digits from 4Q26, while we maintain our 11.6%/5.6% 
GDP forecasts for 2026/27. 
• Inflation is likely to remain above 2% in 4Q26, with our 
2.0%/2.1% CPI forecasts for 2026/27 unchanged. 
• We maintain our call for CBC to hike 12.5bp to 2.125% 
in December, amid tighter liquidity conditions. 
• Housing should recover gradually despite credit 
easing, as excess new -home inventory remains a n 
overhang.

### Page 2

Taiwan: AI, energy and 4Q outlook October 13, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            2

Global backdrop

The global landscape remains complex, 
shaped by the interplay of AI, energy 
prices and tariffs, with significant 
implications for growth, inflation and 
financial conditions.

1) AI

The debate over the durability of the AI 
investment boom continues.  Tailwinds 
include technological advances, broader 
adoption, and s trategic and geopolitical 
considerations. Headwinds range from 
uncertainty over investment returns  and 
hyperscaler financing pressure to 
physical bottlenecks , safety concerns, 
and regulatory uncertainty.

Hyperscaler financing pressure has 
attracted increasing attention . In the 
early stages of the AI investment boom, 
hyperscaler capex was largely financed 
by internal cash flow. This is now 
changing, as free cash flow is expected 
to turn negative from 4Q, requiring major 
players to rely on debt issuance and 
other external financing increasingly.

At the same time, higher global interest 
rates are raising the cost of AI 
investment. The Fed resumed rate hikes 
at its September meeting, while global 
bond yields remain under upward 
pressure amid persistent inflation and 
elevated public and private borro wing 
needs. Our baseline assumes US rates 
will rise by 50bps this year and a further 
25bps in 2027 to 4.50%, with global 
long-term rates also remaining under 
pressure. This could increasingly weigh 
on the economics and pace of AI 
investment.

US-China AI competition is also drawing 
increasing attention.  Across the five 
layers of the AI ecosystem, China is 
making rapid progress in energy, 
infrastructure, AI models and 
applications, supported by its large scale, 
cost-effective domestic manufacturing 
supply chains and huge consumer 
market, which provides a wide range of 
real-world scenarios for AI adoption. The 
emergence of a more integrated and 
cost-competitive Chinese AI ecosystem 
could increasingly challe nge the US 
ecosystem, which currently operates at a 
relatively higher cost.

China, however, remains relatively weak 
in semiconductors.  Around half of 
China's domestic AI chip market still 
relies on foreign suppliers, including 
lower-end NVIDIA chips. This 
semiconductor bottleneck remains a key 
constraint on China's AI development. In 
our view, Taiwanese semiconductor 
companies' competiti ve moat therefore 
remains largely intact in the near term.

2) Energy

The US -Iran conflict remains at a 
standoff, keeping the energy -price 
outlook highly uncertain.  New 
developments are likely after the US 
midterm elections in November, with 
both further escalation and de -
escalation possible. Crude oil prices have 
remained elevated at around 
USD100/bbl amid a persistent 
geopolitical risk premium . In contrast, 
refined product prices have risen more 
sharply as refining disruptions and 
tighter product logistics have amplified 
the supply shock. Natural gas prices 
have also increased sharply, reflecting 
disruptions to LNG supply and limited 
alternative shipping routes.

Taiwan has been actively diversifying 
its energy import sources to cushion the 
impact. LNG imports from Qatar have 
fallen to zero since April, while imports 
from the US and other suppliers have 
surged to offset the shortfall, helping 
maintain stable domestic electricity 
supply.

3) Tariffs

US tariff actions are likely to accelerate 
after the US midterm elections in 
November. Two major tariff measures 
remain in the pipeline. The Section 301 
forced-labor tariff was implemented in 
July, while final tariff action under the 
Section 301 overcapacity investigation, 
which covers 16 economies, remains 
pending. Meanwhile, Phase 1 of the 
Section 232 semiconductor tariffs was 
implemented in January, while Phase 2, 
covering a broader range of 
semiconductor-related products, 
remains pending.

Taiwan has secured a relatively 
favourable framework through its trade 
and investment agreements with the 
US. Taiwan signed an Agreement on 
Reciprocal Trade (ART) and an 
Investment MOU with the US earlier this 
year. The ART establishes a 15% US tariff 
rate on Taiwanese goods, while the MOU 
provides Taiwan with preferential 
treatment under Section 232, including 
for semiconductors. The ART has yet to 
enter into force, and it remains to be 
seen how the final Section 301 tariff 
burden will interact with the 15% ART 
framework. Meanwhile, major Taiwanese 
companies such as TSMC are expanding 
their US investments under the 
Investment MOU, which could support 
relatively favourable treatment for 
Taiwan when broader Section 232 
semiconductor tariffs are introduced.

Taiwan 4Q outlook

Taiwan has navigated the complex 
global environment relatively well so far 
this year, supported by robust AI exports 
and investment, diversified energy 
imports, and efforts to absorb higher 
upstream energy costs. Some 
normalisation is likely in 4Q26 –2027, as 
the AI -driven growth boost moderates 
and domestic energy prices face greater 
pressure to adjust.

1) Growth

GDP growth remains broadly on track 
with our expectations. High -frequency 
indicators suggest that GDP growth 
should remain around 12% yoy in 3Q, 
broadly in line with 12.9% in 2Q but below 
the 15.4% peak in 1Q. Growth is expected 
to moderate to a high -single-digit pace 
from 4Q onwards. We maintain our full -
year GDP growth forecasts at 11.6% for 
2026 and 5.6% for 2027.

The AI cycle remains the key driver of 
Taiwan's growth.  Information and 
communication products and electronic 
components accounted for nearly 80% 
of total exports in the first nine months 
of this year, while manufacturing 
accounts for nearly 50% of gross fixed

capital formation. From 4Q onwards, 
export growth is expected to moderate 
from exceptionally strong levels to a 
more normal but still robust pace, 
broadly following the trajectory of 
global hyperscaler capex and 
semiconductor sales. Investment is likely 
to follow a similar path.

For now, Taiwanese technology 
companies continue to report robust 
revenue growth, led by semiconductors, 
ODMs/EMS, and PCs and servers. 
Capacity utilisation at leading Taiwanese 
foundries has also continued to rise, 
suggesting that investment demand 
remains strong.

The AI boom is also beginning to 
generate broader spillovers to the 
domestic economy. The AI-driven equity 
rally has generated significant wealth 
effects, with the TAIEX up 70% year-to-
date and Taiwan becoming the world's 
fifth-largest stock market. Taiwanese 
households have benefited from the 
appreciation of financial assets, with 
equities and mutual funds accounting for 
nearly 30% of total household financial 
assets. The equity rally has also 
generated positive income effects. Wage 
growth has picked up, led by the services 
sector, particularly financial services, as 
strong earnings at financial 
institutions—especially securities 
firms—have prompted higher 
compensation to retain talent. These 
wealth and income gains should support 
household consumption, creating a 
positive feedback loop that broadens the 
benefits of the AI boom across the wider 
economy.

2) Inflation

CPI inflation is also tracking broadly in 
line with our forecast. Headline CPI 
remained elevated at 2.4% yoy in 3Q, up 
from 1.2% in 1Q and 2.2% in 2Q. We 
maintain our forecast for headline CPI 
inflation to remain close to 2.5% in 4Q, 
and retain our full-year forecasts at 2.0% 
for 2026 and 2.1% for 2027.

Energy inflation remains the main 
upside risk.  The government has 
absorbed much of the increase in energy 
costs through state -owned CPC and 
Taipower, limiting the pass -through to 
domestic consumers. With losses at both 
companies widening, the government 
will need to decide whether to provide 
additional subsidies to cover the losses 
or allow further increases in domestic 
energy prices. The September re view 
postponed the adjustment to the 
December meeting, when the overall 
situation will be reassessed after the 
November local elections. Taipower's  
latest review indicated that electricity 
rates would need to rise by 12.8%. A hike 
in electricity prices could lift inflation 
expectations and generate second -
round effects, particularly as the labour 
market tightens and wage pressures 
increase.

3) Monetary policy

The central bank kept the policy 
discount rate unchanged at 2.00% at its 
September meeting, in line with our 
expectations. We continue to expect a 
modest 12.5bp rate hike at the 
December meeting, bringing the policy 
rate to 2.125%.  By then, CPI inflation is 
likely to have remained above 2% for 
around six consecutive months. The 
possibility of an increase in electricity 
prices following the November local 
elections could further lift inflation 
expectations, strengthening the case for 
a December rate hike.

Financial conditions have already 
tightened despite the absence of a 
policy rate hike.  M2 growth remained 
around 7% yoy in 3Q, well below double-
digit nominal GDP growth. At the same 
time, bank loan growth has accelerated 
to more than 10% yoy, driven by strong

corporate credit demand, particularly AI-
related fundraising by technology 
companies. Tighter financial conditions 
are also evident in rising market interest 
rates, including commercial paper rates 
and government bond yields.

The central bank has continued to ease 
housing credit controls gradually. At its 
September meeting, it raised the LTV 
cap on second -home mortgage loans to 
70%, the second easing of housing credit 
controls this year. There is scope for 
further easing, including removing the 
LTV cap on second-home loans or raising 
the LTV caps for third - and fourth-home 
loans and high -value properties, which 
currently stand at 30%. However, the 
central bank is likely to proceed 
cautiously, given concerns that a faster 
relaxation could reignite  speculative 
demand and put renewed upward 
pressure on housing prices.

4) Housing

A gradual and modest recovery in the 
housing market is expected from 4Q26 
into 2027.  The existing -home market 
has already shown early signs of 
recovery, with Sinyi  housing prices 
returning to slightly positive yoy growth 
since 2Q. Total building transactions also 
returned to positive yoy growth in 3Q. 
The easing of credit controls should help 
reinforce this modest recovery trend.

The new -home market remains under 
pressure. The number of new units under 
construction has started to decline since 
2023, while new completions are  only 
beginning to ease this year . Demand 
remains weak, as potential buyers 
continue to wait for lower prices. Unsold 
new homes stood at 113K units at end -
2025, compared with a historical normal 
level of around 80K, implying excess 
inventory of roughly 30K units. At the 
current pace of new -home transactions, 
it would take roughly one year to 
normalize the existing excess inventory, 
and longer when factoring in the new 
supply pipeline. Some industry experts 
estimate that it could take as long as 3.8 
years digest current inventories fully 
(here).

Document 424: What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-10-11
Pages: 17
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it?
A closer look at the impact of China’s currency on China’s export growth.
Sean Yong/Reuters
UPDATED
October 11, 2026 12:00 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
There is an argument—made in the recent Geneva
Report and by various former IMF officials—that
China’s current export surge is the simple
consequence of the housing downturn and the
resulting weakness in Chinese internal demand.*
The weakness in the RMB in this story is entirely
incidental to the increase in exports, which is, more or
less, a mechanical response to weak internal demand.

Cannot sell excavators at home? Export them.
Built more modern car capacity than your internal
market supports? Export that too…
The fall in the nominal RMB over the course of 2022
and the first half of 2023 in nominal and read terms
was simply a function of domestic macroeconomic
weakness, and a growing rate differential. It wasn’t an
active policy choice by China’s central bank.

### Page 3

3
Now, some might note that China’s currency doesn’t
always move with rate differentials. The RMB didn’t
consistently get pulled up when Chinese rates were
higher than U.S. rates (2002-2020 generally
speaking), and while it did fall when rate differentials
moved against the RMB in 2022 and 2023, it then
stabilized and has even appreciated modestly since
the middle of last year. That appreciation hasn’t been
big enough to correct the deep undervaluation of the

RMB, but it came at a time when the PBOC policy
rate (the reverse repo rate) was well below the U.S.
policy rate.
China doesn’t have an open financial account. As a
result, equilibrium in the foreign exchange market is
thus driven far more by exporter conversion and
current account flows than by rate differentials and
financial flows.** That, together with China’s ongoing
management of the exchange rate and a trade surplus
that can generate significant inflows, means that the

### Page 4

4
value of the RMB is much more of a policy variable
under the independent control of the Chinese
authorities than a simple byproduct of monetary and
fiscal policies.
The modern IMF is reluctant to accept this
conclusion. Its surveillance has generally ignored
China’s currency management (even if that
management is relatively obvious; see the data on FX
settlement) and its recent comprehensive assessment
of China’s economy completely ignored the balance of

payments and exchange rate management.
But it is pretty obvious to the market, even if the
PBOC, rather absurdly, insists that “market forces
play a decisive role in determining the RMB
exchange rate.”
I doubt the PBOC can win this argument. I agree
with Mark Sobel:
“The renminbi is still heavily managed and the
authorities have acted to slow appreciation. The paper
states that the PBoC phased out ‘regular’ foreign
exchange intervention in 2017. But it has many

means at its disposal to achieve the same effect
through extensive capital controls, administrative
guidance, signalling at the fix and deploying state-
owned commercial banks. These means are heavily
used. Thus, the word ‘regular’ must be taken with a
pinch of salt. The comparison to Japan, which

### Page 5

5
intervenes occasionally, is spurious – the yen largely
floats freely in integrated global markets. The same
cannot be said for the renminbi.”
In reality, the PBOC’s daily fix now plays the decisive
role in the currency’s path, and the market is often a
bet on how the PBOC’s management of the yuan will
evolve.
That is clear from the lack of volatility in the yuan’s
rate against the dollar. The yuan’s move down against
the dollar in 2022 was a controlled 10 percent move,

and the appreciation over the last 16 months has been
even more controlled crawl.
And the market is clearly massaged with substantial
help from the state banks. In fact that the pattern of
changes in the monthly FX settlement data looks
exactly like the pattern of intervention of a central
bank managing its currency around a tight band (the

### Page 6

6
state banks in practice buy FX to keep the CNY from
testing the strong side of the band, so there is an
asymmetry in how the CNY is managed)
None of this would matter much if China’s economy
was small. But that obviously isn’t the case. Nor would
this matter much if China’s exports didn’t respond to
changes in the RMB’s real (and nominal) value. But
that is also not the case.
There is abundant evidence that China’s exports do
respond to moves in the currency. Periods of RMB

weakness have consistently been associated with
periods of especially rapid Chinese export growth.

### Page 7

7
Chancellor Merz and President Macron got this
right, even if macroeconomic issues haven’t yet
figured prominently in the EU’s dialogue with
China.*** As they note in their open letter to the
European Commission:
“An appreciation of undervalued currencies towards a value
more in line with fundamentals would help support the
rebalancing of trade accounts with the EU in the short and
medium term … European policy discussions focused on
forging a common understanding of these exchange rate issues

and their interaction with global imbalances would be useful.”
The best evidence that exports responded to changes
in the currency, and not simply to changes in the path
of China’s internal demand, is the acceleration in
China’s export growth after the RMB’s depreciation
over the course of 2022.
This is the killer chart.

### Page 8

8
Now, you might ask: what of the acceleration in
export growth in 2020 and 2021? That was pretty
clearly tied to the global increase in goods demand
that came with the pandemic and work from home
requirements. It was expected to fade when the world
economy normalized. But it didn’t. By 2023 Chinese
export growth again topped global trade growth.
The general expectation that the rise in China’s goods
surplus during Covid would prove to be “transitory”

### Page 9

9
heavily influenced the IMF’s work at the time: see the
IMF’s external sector reports of 2023 and 2024.
I would argue that neglecting the exchange rate was
the key reason the IMF’s kept forecasting, incorrectly,
that China’s current account surplus would fall (See
this blog with Shahin Vallee). The IMF certainly
didn’t warn that China’s property downturn and the
RMB’s real depreciation risked a second China shock
in its 2022, 2023, or 2024 surveillance reports.

Concretely, the weaker RMB appears to have
contributed to a very large outperformance of China’s
exports relative to global trade. See the following
chart
That incidentally also happened after the RMB
followed the dollar down in 2003.
In both periods, there were significant structural
shifts. The WTO accession in 2002, and the success

### Page 10

10
of Chinese industrial policies in the “green” industrial
sectors and autos in 2022. In both cses exports were
turbocharged when structural shifts that should have
resulted in a stronger currency were instead amplified
by currency weakness.
No matter. Paul Krugman has noted that a country
that is converging technologically with its trade
partners should see a trend appreciation in its
currency. China got the technological convergence,
but not the real appreciation—at least not until this

year. And the move since the middle of 2025 has
corrected the depreciation of the RMB that occurred
when the RMB followed the dollar down on
liberation day, but not the structural overvaluation.
Now, there is an argument that exchange rate
adjustment is too hot an issue to make it a priority.
The PBOC isn’t interested it seems.
And some believe that in the interest of comity and
good relations with China, the IMF should mirror
the PBOC’s views. After all a stronger RMB would

slow China’s exports and thus slow China’s economy
at a time when China needs export growth to escape
deflation and generate employment. Or so the
argument goes. Concerns about “external balance“
and the negative global spillovers from China’s rising
goods surplus should be ignored and the Fund’s sole
focus should be on policies that lead to internal

### Page 11

11
balance. That provides one rationale for the Fund’s
ongoing decision to essentially ignore the contentious
issue of the PBOC’s ongoing exchange rate
management.
That argument, however, assumes that China’s
economy is small and thus China’s external surplus
can safely be ignored because it isn’t a drag on the
growth of the IMF’s other members.
That clearly isn’t true.
And it isn’t clear that China is more interested in
having a negotiation over its level of basic pension

benefits or the size of its fiscal deficit (where the
IMF continues to make timid recommendations that
would not move the needle—and certainly wouldn’t
meaningfully reduce China’s external surplus) than a
discussion over its currency practices.****
Back in the world of fixed but adjustable exchange
rates, there was an expectation (not always met to be
sure) that surplus countries would allow their
currencies to strengthen to bring their economies

back into external balance, and that other policy tools
would be used to ensure internal balance.
I would argue that China’s exchange rate (against
both the dollar and the basket) has many similarities
to the old world of fixed but adjustable currencies.
True, it moves on a controlled path when it moves

### Page 12

12
between different levels rather than jumping up and
down. But the PBOC does frequently target a level,
and that targeted level does change over time.
I would further argue that steps like the recent tariff
rate quota that will limit China’s exports of hybrid
cars to Europe to around 350,000 units next year (up
from 2025, but well below the 800,000 units of
2026) aren’t going to address the underlying
imbalance on their own. They can provide a bit of a

buffer for specific sectors.
But that buffer, while necessary, won’t address the
broad-based erosion of manufacturing capacity
outside China. This erosion will persist as long as an
undervalued RMB sends the entire world a signal to
use China as its production base.
The new system of managed trade that is emerging in
autos would need to be progressively expanded to an
ever-increasing set of sectors if protection is used as a
substitute for both domestic reform in China and

exchange rate adjustment.
That is now the direction of travel, but it doesn’t
address the underlying systemic issues.
Incidentally, those who believe the path of internal
demand is critical to rebalancing and that exchange
rate moves are secondary need to explain how an
acceleration in domestic demand would, on its own,

### Page 13

13
lead China’s external accounts to adjust. One
important, stylized fact about China is that domestic
demand growth is no longer feeding over to import
growth (ex-gold, that is).
Bottom line: China, which needs a 1.5 percentage
point contribution from net exports every year, is on a
collision course with its trading partners without a
currency correction that provides the basis for more
balanced trade even in the absence of trade
restrictions.
And there isn’t a realistic path towards balance that

doesn’t involve a stronger RMB; there aren’t, as Paul
Krugman once put it, immaculate transfers.
* That downturn shows up in domestic deflationary
pressure, and weak job growth/high rates of (at least
youth) unemployment. China, in other words, isn’t in
“internal balance.” I really liked the report from

compared with around 45% in normal years, likely reflecting a
shift in market expectations from RMB depreciation to

Document 428: Why China resists rebalancing its growth model_ (2026-10-26)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why China resists rebalancing its growth model_ (2026-10-26).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026EDITORIAL
WHY CHINA RESISTS REBALANCING ITS GROWTH MODEL?
China has never exported as much as it does now, and its trade surplus has never been higher. At the same time, its
economic growth is slowing, held back by weak domestic demand. The imbalances in China’s growth model, and the ex-
ternal imbalances they give rise to, are therefore continuing to worsen. With Europe taking a tougher line and the United

States, with which China shares nothing more than ‘strategic stability’, the standoff looks set to continue. However, the
European Union and China would have a great deal to lose from a lasting deterioration in their relationship. It is in the
interests of both parties to seek to restore the balance. To this end, it is important to understand Beijing’s objectives and
the constraints facing China. Why does the country resist adjusting its growth model? Whilst exports are a vital asset for

sustaining economic activity and asserting China’s technological and industrial power, this strategy also comes at a cost.
IMBALANCES ARE WORSENING
A K-shaped economy. China’s economic growth slowed to 4.6% year-
on-year (y/y) over the first three quarters of 2026, from 5.0% in 2025.
More worryingly, the gap is widening between weak domestic demand
and the very strong performance of exports, driven by artificial intelli-
gence equipment and green technologies. Furthermore, the dynamism

of export-oriented industries has not, so far, had any positive knock-on
effects on other sectors or on households.
Domestic demand is struggling. The property crisis, now in its sixth
year, and a sluggish labour market are weighing on household confi -
dence and consumption. Since the start of 2026, households have been
prioritising debt reduction (bank loans to households have fallen by
1% y/y), house sales have continued to fall, and retail sales have been

stagnating (Chart 1). On the investment front, the decline is becoming
more pronounced and widespread: it is affecting not only the property
sector but also infrastructure and many manufacturing sectors (with
the exception of the textile industry, transport and communications,
and electronic and electrical equipment).
Exports remain very buoyant. Exports continue to be the main driver of
growth (their net contribution is expected to account for around a third

of real GDP growth in 2026, as it did in 2024–2025). Against a backdrop
of rebound in global trade, Chinese companies maintained their market
share at 14.6% of total exports (measured in US dollars) in the first
half of 2026, as they had in 2025. Growth in goods exports accelerated
(21.9% y/y in USD over the first nine months, compared with 5.2% in
2025), driven by both volumes and prices. These have rebounded after
three years of decline, driven by rising costs of raw materials and the

prices of goods required for AI, but they have increased at a slower rate
than the global price average. Exports from high-tech sectors (batte -
ries, electric vehicles, robots and AI equipment) are posting the highest
growth rates (+55.8% y/y for all AI-related goods over the first nine
months of 2026).
Record external surpluses. Imports, which had been stagnating since
2022, have been recovering since the start of the year (up 27.2% y/y
over the first nine months), also driven by both volumes and prices.

The countries benefiting from this rebound in the Chinese market are
commodity exporters and Asian producers integrated into AI value
chains (South Korea in particular). Otherwise, China has significantly
reduced its reliance on imports over the past few years. Overall, the
trade surplus has grown at a slower rate in 2026 than in previous years.
Nevertheless, it has still reached a record high: USD1,249 billion over
the twelve months to September 2026, which is three times its 2019

level (see Chart 2).

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Meanwhile, the current account surplus is expected to approach a re -
cord level of USD 800 billion in 2026, representing nearly 4% of China’s
GDP (a ratio already seen in the late 2000s). As a proportion of global
GDP , this is unprecedented (at 0.6%).
Imbalances set to persist. The imbalances inherent in the growth mo-
del are likely to persist, fuelled notably by the strong price and non-

price competitiveness of Chinese goods. This competitiveness remains
underpinned by: i/ innovation efforts and the move up the value chain
in high-tech sectors and the low-carbon transition; ii/ low production
costs (labour, low-cost credit, land and energy, tax benefits 1); iii/ the
yuan’s exchange rate. Its slow appreciation since mid-2025 has not
offset the cumulative depreciation from 2022 to 2025 (nominal and
real depreciation due to low inflation), and its real effective exchange

rate (REER) remains 10% below its 2021 average. At the end of 2025,
the IMF estimated that the REER was undervalued by between 12%
and 20%.
Beijing’s economic strategy is exacerbating imbalances. The 15th Five-
Year Plan2, launched this year, prioritises innovation, high-tech (ex -
port-oriented) sectors and technological self-reliance above all else,
whilst reforms to the social security system and private consumption
remain on the back burner (see Chart 3). Furthermore, fiscal policy,

which had provided moderate support for household spending for a
time in 2025, was tightened in 2026 (until the recent announcements
of new measures for the final quarter).
WHY BEIJING IS DIGGING ITS HEELS IN
Beijing is not giving ground on any key issues. Faced with trading
partners calling on China to rebalance its economic model, reduce in -
dustrial subsidies and strengthen the yuan, Beijing responds that the
trade surplus reflects the competitiveness of its goods, points to the

United States’ insufficient savings, and uses critical materials as a bar-
gaining chip in negotiations.
The need for growth. Exports are a key driver of growth at a time when
the Chinese economy is undergoing a period of transition. The country
has moved away from its pre-2022 model, which was based on in -
vestment in property and traditional industries and financed by debt.
Growth is now driven by ‘new productive forces’, and exports must pro-

vide producers with the market that domestic demand does not. Fur -
thermore, innovation and the roll-out of AI must stimulate productivity
gains and offset the impact of the shrinking labour force on economic
growth. It is essential to limit the slowdown of growth in order to sup-
port job creation and absorb the economy’s excess debt.
The strategic argument. Exports are a means of asserting China’s in -
dustrial and technological supremacy, weakening its competitors, and

strengthening ‘national security’. They place China at the heart of va -
lue chains in the most strategic industries, such as AI and the low-car-
bon transition. Furthermore, the pursuit of self-sufficiency and import
substitution — which exacerbate external imbalances — also help to
build national sovereignty by reducing dependencies.
A political and fiscal choice. Expanding the social safety net is seen as
a solution for providing long-term support for household consumption3

by reducing their savings rate (estimated at 35.7% of disposable income
in 2025, which is more than double the average rate for the euro area).
1 According to the IMF, the cost of China’s industrial policy amounts to about 4% of GDP . See IMF, ‘Industrial Policy in China: Quantification and Impact on Misalloca-
tion’, August 2025: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229017855.001
2 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, China’s 15th Five-Year Plan: prioritising power over addressing imbalances

3 The IMF estimates, for example, that doubling social expenditure (health, education, social services and basic pensions) in rural areas could reduce savings and
raise the ratio of private consumption to GDP by 1.8 pp in the medium term, at an annual fiscal cost of 1% of GDP . See IMF, People’s Republic of China 2025 Article IV,
February 2026: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229038911.002
However, this is not a priority for Beijing, certainly due to ‘ideologi -

cal’ mistrust of the welfare state and a desire for fiscal prudence.
A major reform of the healthcare or pension systems would place a
strain on public finances in both the short and long term, particularly
as the population ages. Whilst the central government remains in a
sound financial position, local governments are already under strain
following several years of falling land sales proceeds, high deficits and
rising debt. The authorities do not have a large fiscal room for ma -

noeuvre and wish to preserve it in anticipation of future shocks.
LIMITATIONS AND POSSIBLE ADJUSTMENTS
In China, a society gripped by anxiety… Economic imbalances are ta -
king their toll on households. The emphasis placed on manufacturing
rather than services is having a negative impact on employment and
wages (the share of value added allocated to employees’ pay is esti -
mated at 50% in the services sector and 41% in manufacturing). The
downturn in the property market is eroding households’ wealth, and

their savings are mainly held in low-yield bank accounts. Finally, social
spending is rising slowly (by 5.2% a year since 2020, compared with
9.7% over the previous five years).
… and enterprises under pressure. The low prices of Chinese goods,
both for export and on the domestic market — resulting from excess
production capacity and ‘involution’ — are eroding corporate profit
margins. Total profits for industrial enterprises have recovered this
year but they are highly concentrated, particularly in AI-related sec -

tors, while the proportion of industrial companies reporting losses has
continued to rise (30%, compared with 26% in 2022). These vulnerabili-
ties are having increasing contagion effects on the rest of the economy,
notably through worsening payment delays and rising pressure on the
profitability of financial institutions.

### Page 3

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Abroad, trading partners are becoming increasingly intolerant. This
is particularly the case of the United States and the European Union 4,
which have absorbed 24% and 28% respectively of China’s trade surplus
over the last twelve months (data from China’s General Administration
of Customs). Tensions between the US and China could rise again in
2027 when the current truce (which has mainly led to a reduction in

tariffs following the peak in H1 2025) comes to an end. Against a back-
drop of competition for technological supremacy and industrial sove -
reignty, the trend towards decoupling between China and the US could
continue, thereby reducing market opportunities for Chinese producers.
Europe, for its part, is reinforcing its strategic approach and expanding
its toolkit of measures, moving towards more targeted restrictions and
stricter market access conditions, especially in case of serious market

distortions). Rather than pursuing a strategy of decoupling, the EU is
seeking to reduce risks and provide greater protection for critical sec -
tors (including the automotive and chemical industries) without clo -
sing its market and whilst continuing to attract Chinese investment.
This strategy will also lead to tensions. With imbalances becoming
increasingly unsustainable, what course of action would make it pos -
sible to avoid a sudden adjustment precipitated by episodes of crises?

4 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, When it comes to China, Europe should focus less on its trade deficits and more on its investments
A ‘concerted’ adjustment is difficult to envisage. A negotiated adjust -
ment, along the lines of the 1985 Plaza agreements, seems unlikely in
the current international and military context. Furthermore, China and
its trading partners largely disagree on the diagnosis and the causes of
the imbalances. Moreover, a simple revaluation of the yuan would, on

its own, have only a limited effect on China’s competitiveness.
A gradual adjustment may be possible… in the long term. However, the
reduction of imbalances will need to come both from partner countries
(through increased savings) and from China. In the short term, China
can ease trade tensions by investing more abroad, which is all the
easier given that it has huge current account surpluses. This would
enable it to maintain its access to foreign markets and support the

production capacity of its trading partners. More structural changes
(a more flexible exchange rate regime, less fiscal support for indus -
tries and more support for households) will only be feasible once Bei -
jing considers that the costs of its export-led model will outweigh the
strategic gains. A key challenge for trading partners will be to work out
how to shift this cost-benefit balance.
Christine Peltier
christine.peltier@bnpparibas.com

### Page 1

國巨 2327 TT  ( 電子–零組件 ) 
營運槓桿顯現，獲利動能延續 
投資與評價 
1. 國巨 3Q26受惠稼動率提升及前次漲價效益反映，預估毛利率 41.1%、營益
率 29.7%，獲利為近八年新高；4Q26預期隨外溢訂單挹注及最新一輪漲價
逐步反映，營收及營業利益將再創歷史新高。 
2. 國巨 26年受惠 AI需求帶動出貨，交期拉長帶動漲價。預期 26年底村田可
能釋出非蘋 MLCC產能，27年隨日系供應商將產能轉向高階 AI MLCC，供
需缺口進一步擴大，預估 26年 EPS 21.21元、27年 EPS 45.70元。 
3. 目前國巨股價為 547元，約為 12X FY27E EPS，位於近三年 11～50倍 PER
參考區間下緣位置。三星電機 3Q26財報正向進展，更多 LTA簽訂並持續與
客戶協商中，且村田持續傳出 EOL消息。展望後市，預期國巨 AI產品占比
將隨整體 AI需求提升，中階產品外溢訂單持續成長，鉭質電容受惠高市占率
及定價能力，惟市場未能適當反應外溢訂單效應與 27 年漲價幅度，故維持
買進投資評等，微調 27年獲利預估，目標價上修至710元(15x FY27E PER)。 
重點更新 
營運槓桿顯現，預期國巨 3Q26獲利為近八年新高 
國巨 3Q26 受惠前次漲價效益開始反映，加上中階外溢訂單挹注帶動稼動率提
升，3Q26 營收為 495.31 億元(+11.4% QoQ)，與預估之 495.57億元(+11.5% 
QoQ)相近，並優於公司財測之溫和成長幅度。在產品組合改善下，調高毛利率預
估至 41.1%(+2.6 ppts QoQ)，營業費用率則降至 11.4%(-0.7 ppts QoQ)，營運
槓桿顯現，預估營業淨利季增 25.1%至 146.95 億元，營益率 29.7%。稅後淨利
預估為 119.45 億元(+26.8% QoQ)，EPS 5.80 元，毛利率連續第五季上揚，獲
利為近八年新高。

預期國巨 4Q26受惠多重成長動能延續，營收再創新高 
4Q26為客戶庫存檢視節點，客戶後續將增加下單，使供需更趨緊俏，進一步支
撐漲價。然而，以中低階 MLCC為主的消費性需求仍低迷，預期最新一輪漲價貢
獻遞延至 4Q26末。預估 4Q26營收為 534.61億元(+7.9% QoQ)，在稼動率持
續提升及產品組合優化下，上修毛利率預期至 44.2%(+3.1 ppts QoQ)。預期營
業淨利為 175.20億元(+19.2% QoQ)，稅後淨利將季增 19.7%達 142.95億元，
EPS 6.94元，受惠多重成長動能，營收及營業利益將再創歷史新高。

《東協尚未擺脫整理，美印關係改善訊號浮現》
9月MSCI東協指數下跌 2.6%，雖然全球公債殖利率
與油價走升帶來評價面壓力 ，但從股價跌幅及外資
賣超規模來看 ，本波修正仍偏向短線漲多後的技術
性調節。修正後，東協股市本益比已由高於近兩年
平均2倍標準差回落至約 1倍標準差 ，RSI亦降至40
以下，顯示市場過熱情況明顯改善 。然而在聯準會
升息及殖利率維持高檔的背景下 ，若基本面未能提
供更多上修空間 ，預期股市短期仍將維持震盪整理
格局。印度方面，9月回檔已使股價回測 3月低點，
本益比降至 19.9倍，RSI跌破30，顯示短線賣壓已

有過度反應。此外，莫迪於9月金磚峰會公開反對去
美元化，近期川普亦表態支持印歐經濟走廊 ，反映
美印經貿關係出現改善訊號 。後續可持續關注雙邊
合作進展及其對印度中長期投資影響。

《外資9月轉為小幅賣超，呼應8月轉中立看法》
8月下調東協評等至中立 ，主要考量 6至8月股市累
積漲幅已大致反映第三季經濟維持穩健成長的預期 ，
在缺乏新的催化劑下 ，市場進一步上行空間受限 。
進入9月後，美國公債殖利率與國際油價同步走升，
使市場重新評估高利率與成本壓力對企業獲利的影
響，並加大非科技類股及非科技市場的修正幅度 。
不過，相較於美國公債殖利率持續創高所帶來的壓
力，東協股市 9月僅下跌 2.6%，跌幅仍屬溫和；從
資金面來看 ，外資雖轉為淨賣超 ，但賣超規模明顯
低於第二季調整期間 ，顯示本波修正較偏向漲多後
的獲利了結與資產重新配置 ，而非反映市場對區域
基本面的明顯轉弱或出現恐慌性撤資。

《出口支撐成長敘事未變 ，但高通膨也比預期持久 》
東協及印度經濟仍展現韌性，多數國家8月出口維持
雙位數成長且高於長期平均 ，與全球製造業擴張趨
勢相符 ，其中新加坡及馬來西亞受惠 AI強勁資本支
出，8月出口成長年增率逾 5成，顯示外需仍是重要
成長支撐 。強勁的出口與製造活動不僅有助支撐企
業獲利 ，也在一定程度上抵銷高利率對消費與投資
的壓力 。另一方面 ，印尼、菲律賓等先前高通膨國
家雖已進入降溫趨勢 ，但通膨回落速度仍較緩慢 ，
使央行在降息時程上維持審慎 。整體而言 ，目前東
協與印度景氣尚未出現明顯轉弱 ，但高利率環境可
能維持較市場預期更久 ，意味短期經濟成長動能將
更仰賴外需及製造業活動的支撐。

《川普表態支持印歐經濟走廊 ，重構印度樞紐角色 》
川普近期表態支持印歐經濟走廊 (IMEC)，使這項拜
登任內支持，但因加薩戰爭與區域衝突而停滯近3年
的計畫再度受到市場關注 。隨著中東地緣風險升溫 ，
紅海及荷姆茲海峽等關鍵航道不確定性提高 ，各國
對替代性能源與運輸路徑的需求同步增加 。從規劃
路線來看 ，印歐經濟走廊以印度為起點 ，串連海灣
產油國與歐洲市場 ，若計畫順利推進 ，除有助印度
取得更穩定的中東能源供給 、降低對俄羅斯石油的
依賴外 ，也將進一步鞏固其作為全球製造與供應鏈
樞紐的地位 。對印股而言 ，IMEC有望同時強化能源
安全與供應鏈地位 ，鞏固印度在全球製造的地位 ，
並為基建、工業及製造類股帶來中長期題材支撐。

• 川普表態支持擱置近 3年的印
歐經濟走廊重啟 ，在中東地
緣風險升溫下 ，有助印度強
化能源安全 、降低對俄油依
賴，並鞏固全球製造與供應
鏈樞紐地位。

Document 498: ベッセント長官が日本の金融・財政政策を気にする理由

Source PDF: all report pdf/ベッセント長官が日本の金融・財政政策を気にする理由.pdf
Report source: MURC
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

2026 年 10 月 20 日

調査レポート

ベッセント長官が日本の金融・財政政策を気にする理由

～米国の国債市場が抱える潜在リスクと日本への示唆

調査部 研究主幹 中塚伸幸

〇米国のベッセント財務長官が日本の金融･財政政策に「注文」をつける背景には、日本の政策動向が米国の
国債市場に悪影響を及ぼしかねないとの懸念がある。 
〇そもそも米国の連邦政府の赤字は GDP の 6%程度で高止まりしており、フローの観点だけをとれば日本より
状況は厳しい。特に、利払費が歳出の約 14%を占め、大きな負担になっている。財政赤字に加えて、米国債
市場には調達の短期化、ヘッジファンドの保有拡大、民間起債との競合といったリスク要因もある。 
〇日本もいずれ利払費の増加に直面することが見込まれ、ベッセントの「正論」は傾聴に値するといえよう。

1. ベッセント長官は米国債市場への影響を懸念し、日本の政策に「注文」 
米国のベッセント財務長官は 9 月の G20 財務相・中央銀行総裁会議後の会見で「日本はリフレ政策をやめる
べきだ」と発言した。また、これに先立つ日銀･植田総裁との会談では「円安に対処するための断固とした市場･金
融政策を支持する」と伝えた、と米･財務省が公表している。さらに、ニューヨークタイムズ紙は、ベッセント長官が
5 月の片山財務大臣との会食の場で「日銀が利上げすれば円安を是正できる」と大臣に伝えたと報じている。 
米国政府が日本の経済政策に言及・干渉することは珍しいことではないが、ベッセント長官は日本の金融･財
政政策に「注文」をつけている。これは、「米国債のトップセールスマン」を自認する財務長官が、日本の政策動向
が米国債市場に悪影響を及ぼすことを強く懸念しているためと考えられる。それだけ米国の国債市場は不安定
化リスクを内包しているということであり、実際、10 年債の金利は年初の 4.2%程度から足元は 5.2%程度まで上昇
している。日本が円安是正のために米国債を売却するといったことは避けてもらいたく、日本はまずは自らの金
融・財政政策によって円安に対処せよ、ということであろう。 
ベッセントが日本の政策を気にする背景にある、米国債市場のリスク要因について、以下に整理する。 
2. 米国の連邦政府赤字は GDP 比 6%、日本よりも状況は厳しい 
米国の財政状況は厳しい。今月公表された CBO の速報値では、2026 会計年度（25 年 10 月～26 年 9 月、
以下、「年度」）の連邦政府赤字は約 2 兆ドル（1 ドル＝158 円換算で 316 兆円）と、前年比で 12%増加した（次
ページ図表 1）。名目 GDP に対する比率では、26 年度を含めここ数年 6%程度の高水準が続いている。歳出は
パンデミック対応で 20 年度と 21 年度に急増したあと、22 年度以降は縮小したが、24 年度以降は再び増加が続
く。歳入も増加してはいるが、歳出増には及ばず、赤字が拡大している（図表 2）。

### Page 2

歳入･歳出の定義が異なるため日米の比較は難しいが、米国に近づけるために日本の一般会計の歳入から公
債金と前年度剰余金受入を控除し、歳出から債務償還金を控除して収支尻を試算すると、24 年度決算ベースで
日本の中央政府（一般会計）の赤字幅はGDP の 3%程度とみられ、かつ、足もとで比率は改善傾向にある。米国
は GDP の 6%という高水準の赤字が続き、改善の兆しも見られず、日本以上に厳しい状況といえるだろう。

日米の中央政府の歳入・歳出構造の違いとしては、米･連邦政府の収支には日本では社会保障基金に相当
する収支が含まれる点もある。具体的には、歳入にはペイロール税（日本の社会保険料に相当）が含まれ、歳出
にはソーシャル･セキュリティ･ベネフィット（老齢年金給付等）、メディケア（高齢者医療保険給付等）などが含まれ
る。したがって、より平仄を合わせる形で比較するには、中央政府に地方政府と社会保障基金も加えた「一般政
府」ベースで日米を比較することが適切である。この点、IMF の直近の World Economic Outlook Database に
基づいて一般政府ベースの財政収支とグロスの政府債務を比較すると（図表3、4）、政府債務の GDP 比率は日
本が圧倒的に高い一方で、GDP 比でみた財政赤字は米国のほうが日本より厳しい状況にあるといえる。

### Page 3

3. 米国は利払い負担が極めて大きい  
財政収支の悪化は主要先進国に共通する傾向であるが、特に米国の状況が厳しい理由として、利払費負担
が非常に大きいことがある。22 年以降の長期金利上昇に伴って利払費は急増しており、25 年度の利払費（Net 
interest）は歳出全体の 14%を占め、国防費よりもウエイトが高くなっている（図表 5）。利払費は 22 年度から 24
年度まで 3 年連続して前年比 30%以上の伸びであり、25 年度も 10%増、26 年度は若干定義が異なる Net 
interest on public debt の速報ベースで 11%増と、依然として高い伸びが続く。 
 日本の一般会計の利払費（決算ベース）は 24 年度まで 7～8 兆円でほとんど横ばいであるのに対し、米･連邦
政府歳出の利払費は急激に増加しており、大きな違いがある（図表6）。これは、日本の場合は超低金利が長く続
き、その間に調達した債券のウエイトが高いため、債券残高で加重平均した実効金利が低い（ 25 年度末の加重
平均金利は 0.98%）のに対し、米国では金利水準自体が日本より高いうえに、22 年以降急激に長期金利が上昇
したことが影響している（米国債の加重平均金利は 26 年 9 月末で約 3.5%）。 
 ただし、日本の場合も、 10 年債の市場金利はすでに 3%程度まで上昇しており、今後、これに伴う利払費の増
加は避けられない。24 年度までの決算ベースでは図表 6 のようにほぼ横ばいであるが、当初予算ベースでは利
払費は 24 年度の 9.7 兆円から 26 年度には 13.0 兆円へと 2 年間で 3 割以上増加しており、借り換えの進展に
伴ってさらに増加していくものと見込まれる。

### Page 4

4. 法人減税が歳入の伸びを抑制  
 米･連邦政府の歳入は相応に増加している。特に、歳入全体の半分を占める個人所得税は 25 年度に前年比
9%増、26 年度も同 7%増と、着実に伸びている。一方、法人所得税は、歳入全体に占めるウエイトは1 割以下で
あるが、トランプ政権による減税（OBBB 法による一部設備投資の即時償却の恒久化等）により、26 年度は前年
比で 16%も減少している（25 年度も、24 年度には災害等による前年の延期納付分があったことを調整した上で
も、やはり減少）。即時償却によって投資が促進され、将来的に税収増となる可能性はあるが、少なくとも足元はト
ランプ政権の減税政策が歳入を抑えている面は否めない（図表 7、8）。なお、関税も、相互関税が違法とされ還
付となったことから、26 年度は前年比 11%の減少となった。

5. 米国債市場の潜在リスク ～期間の短期化とヘッジファンドによる保有の拡大  
 上述の財政収支そのものの悪化に加えて、米国の国債市場には潜在リスクとなる構造的特徴がある。一つは、
調達が相対的に短期化しており、借り換え対応のプレッシャーが相応に高まっていることである。米国債の平均
残存期間は 5 年 11 カ月と日本の普通国債の平均残存期間 9 年 5 カ月に比べて短い。また、米国債のうち 1 年
以内に償還される債券の比率は 26 年 9 月時点で 33%と、19 年の 27%から上昇しており（次ページ図表 9）、日
本の普通国債の 13%と比べても比率は高い。IMF も、こうした調達短期化の潜在的なリスクを今年 4 月の 4 条
報告で指摘している1。 
 もう一つはヘッジファンドによる保有が拡大していることで、状況によってはそれが市場を不安定化させる懸念
があると指摘されている。FRB は今年 6 月の論文で、国債全体のうちヘッジファンドが保有する比率は 23 年の
約 4.5%から 25 年 9 月には約 8.5%に拡大しており、ヘッジファンドはレバレッジをかけた裁定取引が多いため、
何らかのショックが起きた場合に市場の混乱を増幅させる可能性がある、と警鐘を鳴らしている2。

1 IMF, “United States: 2026 Article IV Consultation,” April 2026 
2 Phillip J. Monin, “Decomposing Hedge Funds’ U.S. Treasury Exposures,” FEDS Notes, June 22, 2026

### Page 5

6. 民間の投資適格社債との競合  
 米国債市場に影響を与える要因としては、民間の投資適格債の起債が増加していることも挙げられる。ハイパ
ースケーラーによるデータセンター建設資金の調達などにより、投資適格社債の発行額は 24 年以降に急増して
おり、26 年も第 3 四半期までの累計額で前年比 25%増と、すでに 25 年通年の実績と同水準に届いている（図
表 10）。こうした投資適格債は比較的安全な資産として米国債と競合するため、国債の需給を緩ませる要因にな
り得る。実際、FRB のウォーシュ議長は 9 月 FOMC 後の会見で、長期金利上昇の要因として、強い景気、地政
学的要因とともに、「資金調達の競争（competition for capital）」を挙げている。

7. 日本への示唆 ～「正論」は傾聴に値する  
 ベッセント長官の「注文」は、内政干渉ともいえるが、しかし正論である。背景にある米国の状況を整理したが、
米国側の事情を抜きにしても、日銀の利上げと財政規律の維持は円安是正の点から日本に必要なことと思われ
る。特に、財政運営においては、足もとはインフレによる税収増に加えて、過去の低利調達の恩恵に負う部分が
大きく、現状約 1%の実効金利はいずれ 3%程度に上昇する。そうなれば、利払費は今よりも 20 兆円膨らむ。さら
に、防衛費や社会保障費の拡大などが見込まれる中、来年度からの食品消費税減税の財源も明らかになってい
ない。ベッセント長官の「助言」は真摯に受け止めるべきであろう。

“ 次贷 ” 的裂缝：来⾃⾦融市场的警报。CoreWeave 信⽤债收益率从上半年的 10% 左右升⾄
15% ，五年期 CDS 利差突破 1100bp ，隐含五年累计⻛险中性违约概率约 60% 。 Neocloud 在信⽤
光谱中处于⾼杠杆、弱现⾦流的脆弱环节，类似 AI 融资体系中的 “ 次贷 ” 式弱资质发⾏⼈：四家上
市 Neocloud 企业 2026 年净债务∕ EBITDA 约 7.5 倍，明显⾼于 6 家⼤型科技企业样本约 0.1 倍的⽔
平。从资产负债率看， 2025 年 Neocloud 样本约 84% ，⾼于⼤型科技企业样本的约 45% 。从现⾦
流看，由于资本投⼊与回收现⾦流时间错配， Neocloud ⾃由现⾦流⻓期处于负区间。

融资压⼒有多⼤？截⾄ 2026 年⼆季度， CoreWeave 已有约 350 亿美元有息债务，其中 29% 为本
次下跌的⽆抵押信⽤债， 39% 为 SPV 承担的有抵押贷款（ DDTL ），但全部债务中 90% 具有⺟公
司追索权。信⽤债收益率上升不直接改变全部存量债务的合同成本，却可能逐步影响后续项⽬
建设与存量债务再融资：我们根据 2.7GW 已签约待建产能、 300 亿美元 /GW 单位投资成本及 20%
的订单预付⽐例，静态估算其仍需约 270 亿美元新增融资， 2031-2032 年另有约 150 亿美元债务
集中到期。
偿债压⼒阈值在哪⾥？我们建⽴五年期 GPU 项⽬融资模型，基准假设下⽆杠杆 IRR 约 15% ⾼于
10% 的融资成本，偿债覆盖率约 1.44 倍，但⾸年偿债覆盖率接近 1.2 倍的预警区域。由于
Neocloud ⾼杠杆、⾼利率、资本开⽀和现⾦流时间错配的特点，贷款成本上升和项⽬现⾦流波
动都会对偿债形成明显压⼒，四种情形下最低偿债覆盖率都将接近 1.0 倍的压⼒阈值：贷款成本

从 10% 升⾄ 15% ，单位年收⼊从 1200 万美元 /MW 降⾄ 1000 万美元 /MW 以下， EBITDA 利润率降
⾄ 60% 以下，或现⾦⽀付 50% 以上集中于最后⼀年。偿债能⼒接近压⼒阈值，借款⼈将⾯临新
增融资压⼒。
⾦融 “ 裂缝 ” 如何传导？当前 CoreWeave ⽆抵押债券⼆级市场到期收益率升⾄ 15% 左右，但存量
DDTL 主要按合同约定利差计息，融资成本并不随⽆抵押债收益率同步重定价。相较⺟公司信⽤
债， DDTL 还具有 GPU 资产抵押和⻓期合同保障，因此融资成本相对较低。但 CoreWeave 约
90% 的有息债务具有追索权，部分仍由⺟公司提供担保。随着⺟公司信⽤恶化，新增 DDTL 融资
条件可能收紧。
是系统性⻛险吗？1 ）我们认为，本轮 AI 融资⻛险是⾼利率环境叠加 AI 放缓担忧下对于 “ 次贷 ” 式
弱资质发⾏⼈的局部信⽤收缩。有抵押 DDTL 成本提⾼幅度可能低于⽆抵押收益率，但⺟公司信
⽤恶化可能进⼀步传导⾄新增项⽬贷款，导致融资条件进⼀步收紧，需要警惕流动性压⼒和偿

债⻛险。 2 ）样本 Neocloud 2026 年资本开⽀预期仅为 6 家⼤型科技公司的 12 分之⼀，⼤型科技
公司的信⽤⽔平仍较为稳健。 AI 债务信⽤质量存在分层，项⽬贷款具有⻓期客⼾合同、资产抵押
及独⽴现⾦流保障；银⾏贷款敞⼝对于资本冲击也有限，我们静态测算单⼀主体敞⼝⻛险对相
关⼤型银⾏ CET-1 充⾜率的直接影响约为 1-5bp 。 3 ）尽管暂时系统性⻛险有限，但⾼利率环境
下如果 AI 景⽓度持续受到抑制，可能引发对于经济增⻓和信⽤周期的担忧，后续重点关注信⽤⻛
险是否沿着 Neocloud 债务向私募信贷等更⼴泛的 AI 债务类别扩散。

AI“ 次贷 ” 的⾦融裂缝
AI 融资⻛险正在遭遇信⽤市场的重新定价。 2026 年 10 ⽉以来， Neocloud 代表企业 CoreWeave 的
多 只 ⾼ 级 ⽆ 抵 押 债 券 到 期收 益 率 升 ⾄ 15% 左 右 ， 五 年 期 信 ⽤ 违 约 互 换 （ CDS ） 利 差 突 破
1100bp 。 Neocloud 债务是 AI 融资体系中的 “ 次贷 ” 吗？融资缺⼝有多⼤，利率上升的压⼒阈值在
哪⾥？⻛险如何传导到⾦融体系？在此前的报告《 AI 的⾦融时刻》中我们探讨了 AI 资本开⽀从内
部现⾦流到外部融资的拐点，本篇我们聚焦 AI 融资中的 Neocloud 债务，以 CoreWeave 为例从信
⽤定价、融资需求、偿债能⼒与⾦融敞⼝四个⻆度评估本轮 AI 融资⻛险。
“ 次贷 ” 的裂缝：来⾃⾦融市场的警报
10 ⽉下旬 CoreWeave 多只⾼级⽆抵押债券价格持续下跌，债券到期收益率升⾄ 15% 左右，显著
⾼于上半年 10% 左右的⽔平。与此同时，公司五年期 CDS 利差⼀度突破 1100bp ，相⽐ 9 ⽉ 800bp

的⽔平明显上升。债券收益率和 CDS 利差同步⾛⾼，反映信⽤市场对 CoreWeave 未来偿债能⼒
的担忧上升。在假设债务违约回收率为 40% ， 1100bp 的五年期 CDS 利差隐含的市场定价五年累
计⻛险中性违约概率约为 60% 。

融资压⼒有多⼤？
存量债务融资成本并不随着⼆级市场到期收益率直接重定价，但存量债务再融资和新增融资成
本可能上升。截⾄ 2026 年⼆季度， CoreWeave 有息债务约 350 亿美元，另有经营租赁负债约 163
亿美元。有息债务中，延迟提款定期贷款（ DDTL ）占⽐ 39% ，相关借款通常由 SPV 承担，并以
GPU 等资产提供担保；近期下跌的⽆抵押信⽤债占⽐ 29% ，其余为可转债等。从债务追索权
看， 90% 的有息债务净额属于有追索权债务，约 10% 属于⽆追索权债务。部分 DDTL 虽然以 SPV
借款并以设备抵押，但由⺟公司提供担保，当⺟公司信⽤状况恶化，有抵押的 DDTL 融资成本也
可能上升。
图表 7 ： CoreWeave 有抵押融资占⽐较⾼，但多数债务具有⺟公司追索权

### Page 5

注：截⾄ 2026 年 6 ⽉ 30 ⽇
资料来源： Bloomberg ，中⾦公司研究部
融资缺⼝有多⼤？根据公司披露的数据，我们采⽤如下静态测算：已锁定算⼒规模约 4.2GW ，
扣除约 1.5GW 在运规模，得到约 2.7GW 待建容量。我们假设单位投资成本约 300 亿美元 /GW 。我
们按此测算，待建签约产能对应总投资约 810 亿美元；扣除已有在建⼯程约 119 亿美元，剩余资
本开⽀成本约 691 亿美元。假设单位年收⼊ 120 亿美元 /GW ，预付资⾦⽐例 20% ，则 2.7GW 待建
容量对应 324 亿美元客⼾预付资⾦，扣除预付资⾦后相关项⽬后续融资需求约 367 亿美元；进⼀
步扣除⼆季度末约 100 亿美元可⽤贷款提款额度，得到静态新增融资需求约 270 亿美元。
图表 8 ：静态测算显⽰， CoreWeave 待建签约产能仍对应约 270 亿美元新增融资需求
注： 4.2GW 合同规模截⾄ 2026 年 8 ⽉，其余数据截⾄ 2026 年 6 ⽉末。假设单位固定资产成本 300

亿美元 /GW ，单位年度收⼊ 120 亿美元 /GW ，预付资⾦⽐例为 20% 。测算未计⼊未来经营现⾦流
及后续融资安排变化
资料来源： Bloomberg ，公司公告，中⾦公司研究部
债券到期压⼒有多⼤？从公司⼆季度末债务结构看， 2031-2032 年为债务集中到期⾼峰，规模约
为 150 亿美元；公司公告其剩余履约义务（ RPO ）将在未来逐步转化为收⼊，但时间与到期再融
资的节奏存在错配，如果债务到期叠加现⾦流下降可能形成再融资压⼒。
图表 9 ：未来债务到期与 RPO 收⼊确认存在期限错配

### Page 6

注：债务到期规模截⾄ 2026 年⼆季度末； RPO 按公司披露的预计确认区间作年度分摊
资料来源： Bloomberg ，公司公告，中⾦公司研究部
偿债压⼒阈值在哪⾥？
为量化测试⻛险情形对偿债能⼒的压⼒，我们建⽴五年期 GPU 项⽬融资模型：假设项⽬容量为
100 MW ，单位年收⼊ 1200 万 /MW ，单位资本开⽀成本为 3000 万美元 /MW ，合同期限为 5 年，五
年均匀确认， EBITDA 利润率为 75% ，资本开⽀中 80% 通过贷款融资， 20% 由股东出资；贷款成
本为 10% ，全部资⾦在期初投⼊，贷款在五年内等额偿还本⾦，剩余部分归属于股东。
根据上述假设，我们测算基准情形下项⽬⽆杠杆 IRR 约 15% ，股权 IRR 约 31% ，五年累计现⾦流 /
贷款本息的偿债覆盖率约 1.44 倍；项⽬⽆杠杆回报⾼于 10% 的贷款成本，使⽤杠杆能够提升股
东回报。由于早期未偿贷款余额较⾼，⾸年偿债覆盖率为 1.25 倍，接近 1.2 倍的预警区域，此后
随着本⾦下降逐步改善。

图表 10 ：数据中⼼ DDTL 模型：基准情形下 5 年⽆杠杆 IRR 约为 15%
注：假设全部资⾦在期初投⼊，贷款在五年内等额偿还本⾦，剩余部分归属于股东。不考虑合
同到期后的经营现⾦流、资产残值、税费
资料来源： Bloomberg ，公司公告，中⾦公司研究部

### Page 7

由于 Neocloud ⾼杠杆、⾼利率、资本开⽀和现⾦流时间错配的特点，贷款成本上升和项⽬现⾦
流波动都会对偿债覆盖率形成压⼒：
1. 考虑到多数 DDTL 存在⺟公司追索权，新增融资成本可能跟随⽆抵押债券上升，但上升幅度可
能略低于信⽤债。当贷款利率从 10% 升⾄ 15% 时，股权 IRR 由约 31% 下降⾄约 16% ，最低偿债覆
盖率由 1.25 倍下降⾄约 1.07 倍，接近 1 倍的压⼒阈值。此时，当股权 IRR 下降，新的资本开⽀项
⽬可能降低对股东的吸引⼒，进⽽导致项⽬推进放缓；偿债能⼒接近压⼒阈值，借款⼈⾯临新
增融资压⼒。降低贷款融资⽐例可以改善偿债覆盖率，但会降低股权 IRR ，并增加股权资⾦筹集
需求。
2. 偿债能⼒对收⼊和利润率较为敏感，现实中多数 AI 基建合同具有⻓期付款承诺，但信⽤资质
较弱的租⼾⽀付具有⼀定不确定性。我们测算单位年收⼊从 1200 万美元 /MW 降⾄ 1000 万美
元 /MW ，或当 EBITDA 利润率低于 60% 时，最低偿债覆盖率会接近 1.0x 的压⼒阈值。项⽬延期或

现⾦⽀付推迟也对投资回报和偿债形成压⼒，我们测算项⽬推迟 3 年及以上股权 IRR 由于利息压
⼒降⾄ 1% 左右，如果现⾦⽀付 50% 以上集中于最后⼀年也会使得最低偿债覆盖率降⾄ 1.0 倍附
近。
图表 11 ：压⼒测试：收⼊、利润率、利率、项⽬交付进度等因素影响偿债能⼒
注：偿债覆盖率 = ⽆杠杆现⾦流 / 贷款还本付息；最低偿债覆盖率为⾸次贷款还本年度的值；每年
贷款本⾦偿还⽐例与收⼊⽐例⼀致；项⽬延期交付假设当年贷款付息不还本

### Page 8

资料来源： Bloomberg ，中⾦公司研究部
⾦融 “ 裂缝 ” 如何传导？
从⽆抵押债务到有抵押项⽬贷款。当前 CoreWeave ⽆抵押债券⼆级市场到期收益率升⾄ 15% 左
右，但存量 DDTL 主要按合同约定利差计息，融资成本并不随⽆抵押债收益率同步重定价。相较
⺟公司信⽤债， DDTL 还具有 GPU 资产抵押和⻓期合同保障，因此融资成本相对较低。但
CoreWeave 约 90% 的有息债务具有追索权，部分仍由⺟公司提供担保。随着⺟公司信⽤恶化，
新增 DDTL 融资条件可能收紧。
⾦融机构敞⼝具有分层特征。截⾄ 2026 年⼆季度， CoreWeave 有息债务约 351 亿美元，主要包
括 DDTL 、⽆抵押⾼级票据、可转债等。近期下跌较多的⾼级票据截⾄ 2026 年⼆季度余额 100 亿
美元，主要为⾮银⾦融机构参与，银⾏敞⼝较⼩。 DDTL 融资由银⾏与⾮银机构共同参与，其中

早期 DDTL 1.0/2.0/2.1 约 75 亿美元，以⾮银机构参与为主； DDTL 3.0/4.0/5.0 及后续新增 DDTL
5.5 合计约 74 亿美元， MS 、 MUFG 、 GS 、 JPM 等银⾏参与。此外，公司循环信贷总授信额度为
25 亿美元，由 MS,MUFG, GS, JPM 等银⾏提供， 2026 年 8 ⽉已提款约 12 亿美元。假设单家银⾏
敞⼝占⽐ 10%-15% ，则单家银⾏敞⼝规模约 9-13 亿美元。

### Page 9

对⼤型银⾏资本的影响。根据公司公告， MS 、 MUFG 、 GS 、 JPM 等银⾏参与部分 DDTL 和循环
信贷融资安排，但实际贷款留存规模未充分披露。假设单家银⾏的⻛险敞⼝ 10 亿美元，损失率
40% ，税率 25% ，如果该部分敞⼝出现损失，按照四家主要相关银⾏（ MS, MUFG, GS, JPM ）
的⻛险加权资产（ RWA ）规模计算，四家银⾏核⼼⼀级资本充⾜率的直接冲击约为 1-5bp 。这意
味着，即使单⼀ Neocloud 借款⼈出现信⽤⻛险，对⼤型银⾏集团资本的直接冲击可能相对有
限。银⾏⼤多数敞⼝为有抵押的 DDTL ，抵押品为芯⽚等资产，在⾼景⽓度周期损失率（ LGD ）
较低，但周期下⾏时存在流动性和折旧⻛险。
图表 14 ：海外⼤型银⾏ CDS 利差上升，反映⾼利率和 Neocloud 债务⻛险
资料来源： Bloomberg ，中⾦公司研究部
图表 15 ： CoreWeave 存量债务情况： 351 亿美元有息债务 +163 亿美元经营租赁债务
注：数据截⾄ 2026 年 2 季度

资料来源： Bloomberg ，公司公告，中⾦公司研究部
是系统性⻛险吗？
⾼利率环境和 AI 放缓担忧下的薄弱环节。我们认为，本轮 AI 融资⻛险是⾼利率环境叠加 AI 放缓担
忧下对于 “ 次贷 ” 式弱资质发⾏⼈的局部信⽤收缩。之所以具有 “ 次贷 ” 特征，在于 Neocloud 普遍存
在⾼杠杆、⾼融资成本、⾼资本开⽀、以及资⾦投⼊与现⾦回收期限错配等问题，对外部融资

### Page 10

条件⾼度敏感。近期 CoreWeave 信⽤债收益率上升⾄ 15% 左右，反映市场已开始重新定价这⼀
融资模式的可持续性。
融资能⼒可能先于项⽬经济性恶化。我们测算 CoreWeave 仍有约 270 亿美元静态新增融资需
求，⽽现有 350 亿美元债务未来亦将陆续到期。根据我们的贷款测算模型，基准项⽬⽆杠杆 IRR
约 15% ，⾼于 10% 的假设贷款成本，但⾸年偿债覆盖率仅 1.25 倍；若贷款成本上升⾄ 15% ，股
权 IRR 由 31% 降⾄ 16% ，⾸年偿债覆盖率接近 1 倍，偿债能⼒接近压⼒阈值。有抵押 DDTL 成本提
⾼幅度可能低于⽆抵押收益率，但⺟公司信⽤恶化可能进⼀步传导⾄新增项⽬贷款，导致融资
条件进⼀步收紧，需要警惕流动性压⼒和偿债⻛险。
局部⻛险的扩散路径。样本 Neocloud 2026 年资本开⽀预期 [2] 仅为 6 家⼤型科技企业 [3] 的 12 分之
⼀，⼤型科技公司的信⽤⽔平仍较为稳健。 AI 债务信⽤质量存在分层，项⽬贷款具有⻓期客⼾合
同、资产抵押及独⽴现⾦流保障；银⾏贷款敞⼝对于资本冲击也有限，我们静态测算单⼀主体

敞⼝⻛险对相关⼤型银⾏ CET-1 充⾜率的直接影响约为 1-5bp 。尽管暂时系统性⻛险有限，但⾼
利率环境下如果 AI 景⽓度持续受到抑制，可能引发对于经济增⻓和信⽤周期的担忧，后续重点关
注信⽤⻛险是否沿着 Neocloud 债务向私募信贷等更⼴泛的 AI 债务类别扩散。

《付鹏说 · 第七季》全新升级上线！⽴即订阅
美股 AI 巨头的 “ ⾼压锅 ” 还能撑多久？看科技巨头的三重⻛险信号
之前⽼法师跟⼤家分享过，信⽤市场永远是领先于市场的，同样市场结构永远是领先于市
场指数的，这些顺序就是联动市场分析的精髓之⼀ , 信⽤市场已经率先反映出对持续资本扩
张、潜在再融资压⼒与现⾦流消耗的敏感情绪，债券与信贷投资者对企业资本分配政策
（狂热投资  vs 股东回报）的容忍度已达临界点 , 形成了典型的 “ ⾦丝雀预警 ” 。
“ 笼⼦⾥的⾦丝雀 ” 是个经典⽐喻：以前矿⼯下井会带⾦丝雀，因为它们对危险⽓体极度敏
感，⼀旦⾦丝雀躁动，矿⼯就知道该逃命了。在⾦融界，信⽤市场（借钱买保险的⼈）⽐
每天炒股的⼈更早嗅到危机。
这张是美股重要的科技公司，尤其是这轮主要 CAPEX 资本⽀出的重要公司的 CDS 信⽤违
约掉期的图表，⼀定是领先的重要的观察数据；这是⾦融市场⾥的 “ 聪明钱 ” 的警钟。

投资全球更要投资⾃⼰

### Page 2

虑本⽂中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

（也就是 CDS ）的成本。简单来说：⼤家觉得这些公司绝对安全时，保费就很便宜；⼀旦
市场隐隐感觉它们可能出问题，保费就会飙升。
你可以清楚地看到，从 2026 年下半年起，⼏家巨头（尤其是 META 和英伟达）的保费直线
上涨。  尽管现在这些科技巨头在股市上可能依然⻛光，但信⽤市场⾥ “ 最聪明的钱 ” 已经开
始害怕了，正在花⼤价钱买保险防⾝。这是在提醒我们，底层⻛险正在聚集，股价是滞后

Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-21
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

十月 21, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新  散熱 ‧ 台灣

雙鴻 
(3324.TW /3324 TT)

新專案與客戶訂單增加，推升液冷營收成長 
重要訊息  
雙鴻 3Q26 營收低於預期，主因泰國 水災及部分專案出貨遞延至 4Q26 。然
液冷業務營收持續成長，預期將帶動 4Q26 至 2027 年 EPS 維持強勁成長。  
評論及分析  
出貨遞延至  4Q26 ；3Q26 營收低於預期。 雙鴻 3Q26 營收季增僅  13%，低
於公司指引及我們預估的  15-20% 成長，主因泰國淹水 及專案出貨遞延， 對 
3Q26 營收的影響約為  4-5 億元，約占 4-5%。由於 訂單未變 ，我們預期 營
收遞延 將帶動 4Q26 營收季增超過  20%。儘管 3Q26 營收低於預期，伺服
器營收比重仍持續提升至  81% ，高於  2Q26 的 78% 。液冷營收占比亦由  
2Q26 及 1H26 的 55% 提升至 3Q26 的 66%，產品組合改善，有利於伺服
器毛利率。然而，由於  PC 與 VGA 營收及產能利用率明顯下滑，預估  3Q26 
毛利率 僅小幅 季增 至約 29.5%，而預估 4Q26 營收強勁將推升毛利率至
30% 。我們據此調整  2026 年營收預估至年增  67% ，主要成長動 能來自伺
服器業務，營收年增  130-140%。我們將  2026 年 EPS 預估調整為  55.01 
元，年增  95%。 
液冷 專案斬獲將 推升  2027 年 營收。 雙鴻  1Q–3Q26 液冷營收 年增  290-
300% ，營收占比提升至  59%，高於  1Q–3Q25 的 26% 。成長動能主要來
自 AI 伺服器需求，雙鴻主力產品為水冷板及內部／機櫃分歧管， 同時有 泰
國 及 中 國 產 能 擴 充 支 撐 。我 們 預 估 液 冷 營 收 今 年 將 年 增約 200% ，並於  
2027 年成長 80-100% ，主要受惠於對  AWS ( 美) 的 ASIC 出貨增加，以 及
對多家 CSP 的分歧管及  CDU 銷售增加。 2H27 潛在新增  CDU 客戶為另一 動
能。而水冷板營收主要 來自  HGX 的 DIMM 與 PCIe ，部分來自  GB ／VR 
Compute Tray 。我們預估  Trainium 3 Max (T3 Max) ( 液冷) 出貨將於  11 月
後量產，而公司可望 2027 年 T3 液冷供應份額 約 20-25%，額外 貢獻營收
約 70-80 億元 (15-20%) 。AWS Trainium 4 目標全面採用液冷設計，相較  
T3 約 40% ，將為 2028 年水冷營收的強勁驅 動力。隨液冷 營收占比預期升
至 2026 年的 62% 、2027 年的 70-75% 及 2028 年的逾 80%，我們預估
毛 利 率 將 由 今 年 的 29.4% 提 升 至 明 年 的 30.2% ， 同 期 營 業 利 益 率 則 由 
17.7% 升至 20.3% 。我們微調  2026 年 EPS 預估至 55.01 元 (年增 95%)，
但上調 2027 年 EPS 至 99.35 元 (年增 81%)。 
投資建議  
我們維持雙鴻「增加持股」評等，因液冷營收增加將推升利潤率與  EPS 成
長，目標價由  1,410 元調升至  1,950 元 (21 倍 2027 年完全稀釋  EPS) 。 
投資風險  
原料價格上漲；筆電、顯卡或伺服器需求疲弱。
